# Sūrah Al-Baqarah (Chapter 2) — Verse-by-Verse Tafsir

## Introduction to the Sūrah

Al-Baqarah is the longest sūrah in the Qur'an, and the books that open it spend their first pages on what it contains rather than on where it sits. The name is the well-known one, and al-Alūsī records the report of Ibn Masʿūd in which the Prophet ﷺ stood at a place and said that this was the station of the one on whom Sūrat al-Baqarah was revealed, which settled the naming for the community. The count of its verses is given as two hundred and eighty-seven on the well-known reading, and al-Baghawī records the same number together with the other counts that were passed down.

Its descent belongs to Madinah, and it did not arrive in one piece. Al-Qurṭubī describes it as revealed over separate stretches of time, and reports that it was the first sūrah sent down after the migration, with one verse as the exception that proves the rule: the command to fear the day of return, which al-Qurṭubī and al-Alūsī both place at the end of the sūrah, was revealed at Minā on the day of sacrifice during the farewell pilgrimage, and the verses on interest came close to the last of the revelation. A sūrah that took years to complete in a community still learning its religion, and that was learned by that community over years, is the reason the books describe the men who carried it as students of it rather than readers of it.

The descriptions the early community left of this sūrah are unusually concrete. Khālid b. Maʿdān called it the pavilion of the Qur'an, a name al-Qurṭubī and al-Alūsī both preserve, and al-Alūsī explains the image from its size and from the number of rulings gathered in it. One of the earliest teachers that Ibn al-ʿArabī heard went further and said that the sūrah holds a thousand commands, a thousand prohibitions, a thousand rulings and a thousand reports, while al-Alūsī records the counting of its parables at fifteen. The same books carry the report that the Prophet ﷺ answered the question of which passage of the Qur'an is greatest with a description of this sūrah as its hump and of the verse of the Throne as the best of it, and the verse of the Throne is inside this sūrah, along with its longest verse, which teaches the writing down of debts.

What the community did with it is part of its story. ʿUmar learned the sūrah with the understanding it carries over twelve years, and his son took eight, which tells a reader more about how the Companions treated this Book than any exhortation could. Al-Qurṭubī reports the report of Abū Umāmah from Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, that the Prophet ﷺ said to recite the sūrah because taking it up is a blessing and leaving it is a grief, and that those who practise sorcery cannot master it, and he also carries the report that a house in which it is recited is a house from which the devil departs. Ibn Kathīr gathers the same material at the head of his commentary, including the report that God does not enter a house with the devil in it where this sūrah is recited, and the report of the expedition in which the youngest man present, who had memorised it, was made commander over his elders, which al-Tirmidhī recorded and graded sound.

How the sūrah stands to the chapter that precedes it is the last thing the books settle before the text. Al-Fātiḥah is a summary in which a servant praises God, confesses his dependence and asks for guidance, and al-Baqarah is the long answer to that request, sent down to a community that had to learn what the guidance looks like in law, in trade, in family and in war. Al-Alūsī puts the connection in one line, that the earlier chapter states the Lordship of God and the servitude of the human being, and this one unfolds what the two mean in practice. So the reader who finished the Fātiḥah asking to be shown the straight path begins the second sūrah watching the Book describe the people who are on it, the people who have turned their backs on it, and the people who stand in between saying one thing and doing another — the three groups the next few verses set out in turn.
---

## Verse 2:1

> Alif-Lãm-Mĩm

**A BOOK THAT OPENS WITH THREE LETTERS**

The sūrah begins with ***“Alif-Lãm-Mĩm”***, three letters of the alphabet standing alone before a word of the Book has been spoken. The classical books treat the silence around them as part of the revelation rather than a gap in the manuscripts. The view al-Baghawī records from al-Shaʿbī and a body of early readers is that these letters belong to the passages whose knowledge God kept for Himself, that they are the secret of the Qur'an, and that the duty of the reader towards them is faith in what they are rather than a settled meaning. The teaching is summed in two sentences the same writer preserves: Abū Bakr said that in every book there is a secret. God's secret in the Qur'an is the openings of its sūrahs, and ʿAlī said that every book has a chosen part, and the chosen part of this Book is its letters. The point is that the two readers closest to the letters said the least about their meaning, which means the silence is a position held by men who could have filled it.

Not every early reader left the letters unexplained, and the reports that do interpret them all proceed by taking each letter as the beginning of something larger. The plainest of them, carried in the reports attributed to Ibn ʿAbbās, reads the three as the opening letters of the names of Allah, of Gabriel and of Muḥammad, so that the letters become a compressed statement of the revelation's three parties: the One who sent it, the angel who carried it and the man who received it. The same reports offer a second reading in which the letters begin words for God's blessings, kindness and dominion. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān records the further views that the letters name the sūrahs they begin or stand as symbols of the divine names. Every one of these readings has the same shape, which is that a letter is a fragment of a word and a word is a fragment of a sentence. What follows is a rule of reading that holds whichever view a person takes, since the letters are recited as text in every case and never skipped as marks on a page.

Where the safest reading lies is a question the books answer with a mixture of caution and confidence, and the mixture is worth copying. Al-Saʿdī writes that the soundest course with the isolated letters is to refrain from assigning a meaning without evidence from revelation. Being certain that God did not send them down as a game: they were sent for a wisdom, even where the wisdom is beyond us. Al-Jalālayn reduces the whole matter to five words, that God knows best what He means by these letters. Al-Ṭabarī records among the early views the position that they are simply a name for the Qur'an as a whole, which is to say a title standing in front of the book it names.

Think of a letter delivered with its first line written in a hand the reader cannot yet decipher, signed at the bottom by someone he trusts absolutely. The sensible reader does not throw the letter away, and he does not invent a text for the first line either; he reads what follows, and he keeps the unfamiliar line open until its meaning is given. The first verse of this sūrah asks for exactly that posture from a reader. The rest of the sūrah, which will spend two hundred and eighty-six verses explaining itself, is where the habit is rewarded. That is the arrangement of the whole chapter in one line: the trust comes first, because the explanations that follow only reach a reader who stayed.

**WHY THE BOOK BEGINS BY WITHHOLDING SOMETHING**

A book that opens by withholding meaning from its first line is making a claim about its reader before it makes a claim about God, and the early community noticed the boldness of it. The verses that follow this one describe a Book in which there is no doubt, and the doubt-free Book is the one whose very first word declines to explain itself. Al-Saʿdī's point that the letters were not sent in play is the hinge here, because it separates two ways of reading the line: either the letters mean nothing and the Book began with noise, or they mean something that its readers have not been given. The difference between those two readings is the difference between trusting the speaker and trusting only the words one already understands. The point is that doubt is answered by the speaker rather than by the reader's grasp, which is why the chapter that claims there is no doubt in the Book begins with three letters no reader can settle.

The pragmatics of the line are as interesting as its meaning, and al-Baghawī's note on the benefit of the letters is the place to see it. He records that the benefit of mentioning them is the demand to believe in them, which is to say the line exists to be accepted rather than to be solved. A religion whose Book starts in that way has already begun teaching the discipline it will ask for later: a person who cannot submit to a text before he has mastered it will not submit to anything that outruns him. Everything the Book says about God will outrun him.

Read with what comes next, the letters also fix the register of the sūrah. This is the chapter that will legislate for a community — prayer in its verses, fasting, pilgrimage, debt, marriage, war and the rules of lending — and it opens not with law but with three letters no one can decode. The order tells a reader that the law is not the foundation; the speaker is. That is why the sūrah's first sentence after the letters is about the Book as guidance rather than about a ruling. Why the qualities it praises in its readers at verses three to five are qualities of belief before they are qualities of behaviour.

Imagine an architect who hands over a set of drawings and, on the first sheet, a symbol whose meaning only he knows. The builder who trusts the architect lays the foundation and carries on to the pages he can read, and the symbol is answered by the finished building rather than by a footnote. The isolated letters are that first sheet, in a book whose author will be sending the rest of the pages for the next two hundred and eighty-six verses. The best reader of the sūrah is the one who keeps reading after the symbol.

---

## Verse 2:2

> This is the Book! There is no doubt about it—a guide for those mindful ˹of Allah˺

**A DEMONSTRATIVE THAT POINTS BACKWARD**

The sūrah describes itself with ***“This is the Book!”***, and the first word of that description is the one the books discuss. Arabic has near and far demonstratives, and the far one is used here for a Book that is present in the mouth of the Prophet ﷺ and in the memory of everyone listening. Al-Ṭabarī gathers the early readings of the word and reports that the run of the exegetes took it as meaning this Book, with the explanation carried from Mujāhid, ʿIkrima and al-Suddī through chains he cites by name. Al-Jalālayn gives the reason for the distance: the demonstrative is there to magnify what it points at, so that the Book is lifted out of the hands of its audience before it is described.

A second reading of the distance is subtler and links this sūrah to the one before it. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the far demonstrative can be read as pointing back to the request with which the Fātiḥah ends, the plea to be shown the straight path, so that the words work as an answer rather than an introduction: this is the Book you asked for. On that reading the two chapters are joined at the seam, and the long sūrah begins inside a conversation that the short one started. The reader who has just asked for guidance is handed a Book and told what it does.

What the opening does for the reader is worth stopping over, because it inverts the way books usually behave. A preface normally tells a reader what he is holding and then asks for his patience. Here the reader is told that the thing he requested has arrived, before he is given one detail about what it contains. That is why the phrase has the force of a delivery rather than a description. Why the verses that follow read as a specification of the gift — who benefits from it, and what they do with it — instead of a table of contents.

Think of a man who has spent a long time asking a doctor for something to cure him and receives, without a word of explanation, a sealed box with a name on it. He does not complain that the box has not described its contents; he opens it, because the answer was the thing he asked for. The opening of this sūrah is that moment in words, placed directly after the request, and everything in the next two hundred and eighty verses is the contents of the box described.

**NO DOUBT IN IT, AND WHAT A DENIAL CARRIES**

The second claim of the verse is ***“There is no doubt about it”***, and the word at its centre is the technical one. Ibn Kathīr explains it as doubt and uncertainty, and he records that the earliest readers agreed on the sense without argument: the statement is carried in Ibn Kathīr's commentary from Ibn ʿAbbās, from Ibn Masʿūd and from a list of Companions and Successors whose names he gives. Ibn Abī Ḥātim is quoted saying that he knows of no disagreement over the meaning. A denial that attracted no dispute among the people closest to the revelation is itself part of the evidence.

What such a denial implies is the part of the verse that repays attention. Al-Saʿdī lays down a rule of Arabic rhetoric and applies it here: when a fault is denied for the purpose of praise, the denial carries the opposite quality as well. The mere absence of a fault is not praise. So the sentence does not only say that doubt is missing; it says that certainty is present, and the Book contains knowledge that settles the matter it speaks about. That is why the negation is worth more than a compliment about the text's quality, and why the claim is about the Book's origin rather than the reader's mood.

The Book also shows where doubt comes from and how it is answered, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān sets out both possibilities. A statement can be doubtful because it is wrong, or because the listener has understood it wrongly, and the second case does not make the statement doubtful. The Book, on that reading, is sure in itself while the difficulty belongs to the understanding of the doubtful. The same sūrah then does the honest thing and invites the doubter to bring his evidence, issuing the challenge a few verses later to produce a single sūrah of its like. The certainty claimed at verse two is therefore offered for testing at verse twenty-three, not merely asserted.

Imagine two men examining the same bridge: one walks across it, the other stands at the entrance pressing on a single railing and declaring the whole structure unsound. The builder's claim about his bridge is not disproved by the second man's posture, and the test he offers is not a debate about railings but a crossing. That is the difference this verse draws between doubt that belongs to the thing and doubt that belongs to the one looking at it, and it is why the Book can call itself certain while its audience includes people who are not.

**GUIDANCE THAT FINDS THE GUARDED**

The verse ends with ***“a guide for those mindful ˹of Allah˺”***, and the books read the closing phrase as a statement about how guidance works rather than as a flattery of the Book. Al-Ṭabarī draws the contrast sharply: the same Book is guidance and healing for the mindful, while for those who deny it, it is a weight and a proof against them. If the light reached everyone alike, he argues, God would not have singled out the mindful as its beneficiaries. Guidance here is not a fact about paper but a use made of it, and the Book does different things in different hands.

Who the mindful are is a question the books answer with a definition and a ladder. Al-Alūsī traces the word to a root meaning protection and defines the quality as a person guarding himself against what will harm him in the life to come, and then he sets out the ranks: the first is guarding against worshipping anything with God, the next against the major sins and persistence in the minor ones. The highest is the level described in the report al-Alūsī quotes from al-Tirmidhī, that a person does not arrive among the mindful until he leaves even what is harmless for fear of what is not. The ranks explain why the word covers both a beginner and a prophet.

Reading the two halves of the verse together shows why the order is what it is. A Book that carries certainty is offered first, and the disposition that benefits from it is named second. The reader is not told to become something before he can look, only that looking will cost him something. Al-Saʿdī makes the same point from the side of the reader with a sentence that has the shape of a rule, that guidance only reaches the one who seeks it and is willing to follow where it leads. The verse therefore ends where the reader's own decision begins.

Think of a house with its windows shut against the mid-day sun. The light is not diminished by the closed shutters, and no one living in the house is lit while they stay closed. Opening a shutter adds nothing to the sun and changes everything in the room. The phrase at the end of this verse describes the opening rather than the light: a reader who guards himself against what harms him has already begun letting the Book do what the Book does.

---

## Verse 2:3

> who believe in the unseen, establish prayer, and donate from what We have provided for them

**FAITH IN WHAT CANNOT BE CHECKED**

The verse introduces the people the Book guides with ***“who believe in the unseen, establish prayer”***, and the first of the two qualities is the one that cannot be verified by looking. Al-Jalālayn lists what the unseen covers in the plainest terms, the resurrection, the garden and the fire, all of them matters no one has seen and no one can inspect. Ibn ʿAbbās, in the report al-Ṭabarī carries, gives the sense a wider reach still: the unseen is what has come from God. Makes the object of faith everything He has reported rather than a short list of hidden things.

Why the unseen is the test rather than a detail is the argument al-Saʿdī builds here, and it is worth following closely. Faith in what a person can see distinguishes nobody, since believer and denier both accept a sunrise. The thing that separates them has to be a report accepted on the speaker's authority alone. The believer, on that account, believes what God and His messenger have said whether he has seen it or not. Whether his own understanding can reach it or not, since the affirmation is of the speaker rather than of the evidence. A person who only believes what he can check has placed his own judgement above the report he claims to accept. The point is that the object of faith is a speaker rather than a fact, which is why the verse names what cannot be seen before it names anything a person does.

What the word for faith carries in the Book is wider than mental agreement, and the early readers left several descriptions of it that Ibn Kathīr collects. ʿAbdullāh b. Masʿūd said that faith is to trust, and Ibn ʿAbbās is reported with the same sense; al-Zuhrī described faith as the deeds themselves; al-Rabīʿ b. Anas read the phrase as meaning that they fear God. Al-Ṭabarī's own preference, stated after weighing the reports, is that the people described here affirm the unseen with tongue, belief and action together, and he refuses to narrow the word to one of its parts. The list matters because it stops the reader from deciding that one of the three will do.

Imagine a sailor who has never seen the far shore and sets his course by a chart and by the word of a captain who has made the crossing many times. His trust is not blind, since the chart is in his hands and the captain's record is known, and it is not sight either. The harbour he is steering for remains invisible the whole way. That is the position the first quality of this verse describes. The Book's own remark that such faith distinguishes a believer from a denier explains why it is named before prayer and before spending.

**PRAYER SET UP SO THAT IT STANDS**

The second quality is ***“who believe in the unseen, establish prayer”*** as far as the phrase about prayer, and the verb chosen for it is not the ordinary word for performing a duty. Al-Qurṭubī works through the root and notes that the word is used of something standing firm and continuing, the way one says that the truth stood when it became established, and that the form of the verb here carries the sense of keeping something upright rather than finishing it once. His definition of the duty itself is equally concrete: performing the prayer with its pillars, its practices and its postures, each at its own time.

The difference between setting up a prayer and getting a prayer over with is the whole force of the word, and the books give a memorable reading of it. Al-Qurṭubī records the statement of ʿUmar b. al-Khaṭṭāb about the one who preserves the prayer and keeps watch over it, treating the preservation as the mark of the person rather than the performance. That is the reverse of how the duty is usually described. A traveller can satisfy it in a few minutes and a sick man lying down, and the Book still asks them to stand it up. The claim is about a life held in a certain shape, not about a slot in a timetable.

The pairing of the two qualities also explains why the unseen comes first. A person who has accepted a report about what he cannot see has already given up the position from which prayer looks pointless. Prayer is an act addressed to a speaker no one in the room can point at. Al-Jalālayn's gloss on the phrase keeps the two joined in a single breath, faith in what is hidden and the due performance of the prayer. The order in the verse is the order of the argument: first the trust, then the act that only trust explains.

Think of a man who agrees to look after a house for an owner he has never met, and who opens the place every morning, waters what needs watering and keeps the accounts in order. Anyone watching sees only a routine, and the routine is intelligible only if the absent owner is real to him. Prayer is that daily opening of the house, and the verse places it directly after a description of faith in someone who is not visible.

**SPENDING OUT OF WHAT WAS NEVER MINE**

The third quality completes the verse: ***“and donate from what We have provided for them”***. The preposition at the heart of the phrase does quiet work, since it says from what We have provided and not all of it, so that the claim is a share rather than a surrender of everything. Al-Jalālayn reads the whole phrase as spending in obedience to God. Keeps the gift inside the relationship the verse has been building: the same hands that were given the wealth are asked to pass part of it on in the name of its owner.

The order of the three qualities is where the verse's logic becomes visible. Belief turns a person towards what he cannot see, prayer puts his day under that belief. Giving away visible property tests both, because a coin in the hand is the most checkable thing a person owns. A belief that costs a man nothing has not yet been asked the hard question, and the third quality is the question. Al-Baghawī preserves a report in this place that the community repeated as a summary, that no one has believed with a faith better than faith in the unseen, and he read the whole description of the believers from the letters at the start of the sūrah down to the promise at its fifth verse.

What the verse does not do is name the amount, the object or the recipient, and the omission is deliberate in a Book that is careful elsewhere to legislate in detail. Al-Saʿdī's handling of the description keeps it at the level of quality rather than quantity for the same reason: a believer's spending is evidence of his belief in proportion to how much his belief moves him. The obligatory share of wealth comes later in this very sūrah with its rates set out, and the general word chosen here leaves the habit in place of the figure. That is why the verse can leave the amount open without weakening the duty, since what a person gives is measured against what he was provided.

Picture a steward who is handed the keys to a warehouse he did not build and did not pay for. He sells nothing of his own and gives nothing of his own. When he hands out grain to a household in need he writes it in the owner's book rather than his own. The verse's phrase about what We have provided puts every reader in that steward's position. The generosity it asks for is easier to understand once the ownership question is settled, which is exactly what the words about the unseen and the God who provides have done in the two clauses before it.

## Verse 2:187

> It has been made permissible for you to be intimate with your wives during the nights preceding the fast. Your spouses are a garment for you as you are for them. Allah knows that you were deceiving yourselves. So He has accepted your repentance and pardoned you. So now you may be intimate with them and seek what Allah has prescribed for you. ˹You may˺ eat and drink until you see the light of dawn breaking the darkness of night, then complete the fast until nightfall. Do not be intimate with your spouses while you are meditating in the mosques. These are the limits set by Allah, so do not exceed them. This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to people, so they may become mindful ˹of Him˺

## Verse 2:196

> Complete the pilgrimage and minor pilgrimage for Allah. But if prevented ˹from proceeding˺, then ˹offer˺ whatever sacrificial animals you can afford. And do not shave your heads until the sacrificial animal reaches its destination. But if any of you is ill or has a scalp ailment ˹requiring shaving˺, then compensate either by fasting, charity, or a sacrificial offering. In times of peace, you may combine the pilgrimage and minor pilgrimage then make the sacrificial offering you can afford. Whoever cannot afford that ˹offering˺, let them fast three days during pilgrimage and seven after returning ˹home˺—completing ten. These offerings are for those who do not live near the Sacred House. ## Verse 2:217

> They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ about fighting in the sacred months. Say, “Fighting during these months is a great sin. But hindering ˹others˺ from the Path of Allah, rejecting Him, and expelling the worshippers from the Sacred Mosque is ˹a˺ greater ˹sin˺ in the sight of Allah. For persecution is far worse than killing. And they will not stop fighting you until they turn you away from your faith—if they can. And whoever among you renounces their own faith and dies a disbeliever, their deeds will become void in this life and in the Hereafter. It is they who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.”

## Verse 2:219

## Verse 2:233

> ˹Divorced˺ mothers will breastfeed their offspring for two whole years, for those who wish to complete the nursing ˹of their child˺. The child’s father will provide reasonable maintenance and clothing for the mother ˹during that period˺. No one will be charged with more than they can bear. No mother or father should be made to suffer for their child. The ˹father’s˺ heirs are under the same obligation. But if both sides decide—after mutual consultation and consent—to wean a child, then there is no blame on them. If you decide to have your children nursed by a wet-nurse, it is permissible as long as you pay fairly. Be mindful of Allah, and know that Allah is All-Seeing of what you do

## Verse 2:259

> Or ˹are you not aware of˺ the one who passed by a city which was in ruins. He wondered, “How could Allah bring this back to life after its destruction?” So Allah caused him to die for a hundred years then brought him back to life. Allah asked, “How long have you remained ˹in this state˺?” He replied, “Perhaps a day or part of a day.” Allah said, “No! You have remained here for a hundred years! Just look at your food and drink—they have not spoiled. ˹But now˺ look at ˹the remains of˺ your donkey! And ˹so˺ We have made you into a sign for humanity. And look at the bones ˹of the donkey˺, how We bring them together then clothe them with flesh!” When this was made clear to him, he declared, “˹Now˺ I know that Allah is Most Capable of everything.”

## Verse 2:282

> O believers! When you contract a loan for a fixed period of time, commit it to writing. Let the scribe maintain justice between the parties. The scribe should not refuse to write as Allah has taught them to write. They will write what the debtor dictates, bearing Allah in mind and not defrauding the debt. If the debtor is incompetent, weak, or unable to dictate, let their guardian dictate for them with justice. Call upon two of your men to witness. If two men cannot be found, then one man and two women of your choice will witness—so if one of the women forgets the other may remind her. The witnesses must not refuse when they are summoned. You must not be against writing ˹contracts˺ for a fixed period—whether the sum is small or great. This is more just ˹for you˺ in the sight of Allah, and more convenient to establish evidence and remove doubts. However, if you conduct an immediate transaction among yourselves, then there is no need for you to record it, but call upon witnesses when a deal is finalized. Let no harm come to the scribe or witnesses. If you do, then you have gravely exceeded ˹your limits˺. Be mindful of Allah, for Allah ˹is the One Who˺ teaches you. And Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of all things

## Verse 2:286

> Allah does not require of any soul more than what it can afford. All good will be for its own benefit, and all evil will be to its own loss. ˹The believers pray,˺ “Our Lord! Do not punish us if we forget or make a mistake. Our Lord! Do not place a burden on us like the one you placed on those before us. Our Lord! Do not burden us with what we cannot bear. Pardon us, forgive us, and have mercy on us. You are our ˹only˺ Guardian. So grant us victory over the disbelieving people.”
